But for simplicity and correctness, let me start with the more explicit version and see. Now, one important detail: when loading K and V from global memory, I need to ensure coalesced access. The global memory layout is [batch, head, seq, head_size]. For K[batch, kv_head, kv_start + n, d] with n varying across threads. If each thread loads one K element: `k_tile[n * HEAD_SIZE + d] = K[batch_idx * kv_batch_stride + kv_head * kv_head_stride + (kv_start + n) * HEAD_SIZE + d]` With 128 threads, if consecutive threads load consecutive elements, the access is coalesced. For tid=0: n=0, d=0 For tid=1: n=0, d=1 ... For tid=127: n=0, d=127 ? Or n=1, d=0 ? If I assign `idx = tid`, then element `(n, d)` where `n = idx / HEAD_SIZE`, `d = idx % HEAD_SIZE`. For tid=0: n=0, d=0 For tid=1: n=0, d=1 ... For tid=127: n=0, d=127 Wait, for BLOCK_N=128 and HEAD_SIZE=128, BLOCK_N * HEAD_SIZE = 16384. With 128 threads, each thread would handle `16384 / 128 = 128` elements. For `idx = tid + i * blockDim.x` with i going 0..127: i=0: idx=tid → n=0, d=tid for tid=0..127 i=1: idx=128+tid → n=1, d=tid for tid=0..127 ... So thread tid loads: - (n=0, d=tid) - (n=1, d=tid) - (n=2, d=tid) - ... For tid=0: loads (0,0), (1,0), (2,0), ... For tid=1: loads (0,1), (1,1), (2,1), ... In global memory, K[batch, head, seq, d] is stored with seq as the fastest varying dimension after d. So consecutive d values for the same seq are adjacent. Thread 0 loads K[..., 0, 0], K[..., 1, 0], K[..., 2, 0], ... These are spaced by HEAD_SIZE * sizeof(half) = 256 bytes. Not coalesced. Thread 1 loads K[..., 0, 1], K[..., 1, 1], ... These are adjacent to thread 0's first load (differ by 2 bytes). But thread 0 and thread 1 are in the same warp. For global memory, a warp's load is coalesced if the addresses are contiguous. For threads 0..31 loading (n=0, d=tid): - Thread 0: address = base + 0 - Thread 1: address = base + 2 - ... - Thread 31: address = base + 62 These are contiguous 2-byte elements (64 bytes total). This is coalesced into one 64-byte transaction. Good! Then in the next iteration (i=1), threads load (n=1, d=tid): - Thread 0: address = base + 256 - Thread 1: address = base + 258 Again contiguous. Good! So the global memory loads ARE coalesced. Great. For K_tile layout in shared memory: `k_tile[n * HEAD_SIZE + d]`. When thread tid accesses `k_tile[tid * HEAD_SIZE + d]` for dot product computation: Address = (tid * HEAD_SIZE + d) * 2 bytes. For d fixed and tid varying: addresses are `2d, 2d + 256, 2d + 512, ...`. Banks: `(2d + 256*tid) / 4 % 32 = (d/2 + 64*tid) % 32 = d/2 % 32` (since 64*tid % 32 = 0). So ALL threads access the SAME bank! This is a 128-way bank conflict for the K tile read during dot product computation! Oh no, this is terrible for performance. My original kernel avoided this by using a strided n pattern. But here, each thread is assigned a distinct n, so during dot product, all threads read different n but same d, causing massive bank conflicts. To fix this, I can transpose the K data in shared memory: Store K_tile as [HEAD_SIZE][BLOCK_N] instead of [BLOCK_N][HEAD_SIZE]. Then `k_tile[d][tid]` is at address `(d * BLOCK_N + tid) * 2`. Banks: `(d * BLOCK_N + tid) / 2 % 32 = (d * BLOCK_N / 2 + tid / 2) % 32`. For BLOCK_N=128: `d * 64 + tid / 2`. Mod 32: `(tid / 2) % 32` (since d*64 is multiple of 32). For tid=0..31: banks = 0, 0, 1, 1, 2, 2, ..., 15, 15. This is a 2-way conflict. For tid=32..63: banks = 16, 16, 17, 17, ..., 31, 31. Also 2-way conflict. But wait, threads 0 and 1 both access bank 0, but DIFFERENT addresses (d*128+0 vs d*128+1, which are 2 bytes apart, in the same 4-byte word). So it's not a conflict; the hardware handles it. Actually, threads 0 and 1 access addresses that are 2 bytes apart. On Ampere/Hopper, shared memory banks are 4 bytes wide. Two 2-byte accesses in the same 4-byte word can be serviced in a single transaction if they're in the same bank. This is NOT a conflict. What about threads 0 and 32? Thread 0: address = d*128 + 0, bank = 0 Thread 32: address = d*128 + 32, bank = 16 Different banks. No conflict. So with transposed layout, we have no bank conflicts for dot product! But when LOADING K into shared memory, we might have conflicts. When loading K into transposed shared memory: We want to store K_tile[d][n] = K_global[..., n, d]. If thread tid loads K_global[..., n=tid, d=i] for different i, it writes to smem[d=i][n=tid]. For `i=0`: thread tid writes to address `0 * BLOCK_N + tid = tid`. For `i=1`: thread tid writes to address `1 * BLOCK_N + tid = BLOCK_N + tid = 128 + tid`. Banks: `address / 2 % 32 = tid / 2 % 32` for i=0, and `(128 + tid) / 2 % 32 = (64 + tid/2) % 32 = tid/2 % 32`. Both i=0 and i=1 have the same bank pattern. Since threads write to different banks (for even/odd tid pairs sharing a bank), and writes in the same warp but different banks can proceed in parallel, there are no conflicts. Actually, for `tid=0`: bank 0, address 0 For `tid=1`: bank 0, address 1 (same 4-byte word) For `tid=2`: bank 1, address 2 Threads 0 and 1 write to the same bank and same 4-byte word. Since they write to different halves, the hardware can handle this (2-byte writes to the same 4-byte word are merged). No conflict. OK, so transposed K tile in shared memory gives: - No bank conflicts during dot product - No bank conflicts during loading - But requires transposed indexing For V tile, when we do PV multiply, we read v_tile[n][d]. With transposed layout v_tile[d][n], we'd read by iterating n: ```cpp for (int n = 0; n < valid_n; ++n) { o_local += p_smem[n] * __half2float(v_tile[d][n]); } ``` This access pattern for v_tile is: `v_tile[d][n]` with d fixed (tid) and n varying. Address = (d * BLOCK_N + n) * 2. Banks: (d * BLOCK_N / 2 + n / 2) % 32. With BLOCK_N=128: banks = n/2 % 32 (since d*64 is multiple of 32). For n=0: bank 0, n=1: bank 0, n=2: bank 1, n=3: bank 1, ... As n increases by 1 in the loop, n/2 increases by 0 (for odd n) or 1 (for even n). But this is sequential within a thread, not across threads in a warp. Bank conflicts only matter for SIMULTANEOUS access by threads in the same warp. For the PV multiply, each thread has its own loop over n. Since threads in a warp are executing the same loop but at potentially different n values... wait, they all start at n=0 and increment. At iteration n: Thread 0: reads v_tile[d][0], bank = 0 Thread 1: reads v_tile[d][1], bank = 0 Thread 2: reads v_tile[d][2], bank = 1 ... For a warp, at n=0: threads 0..31 read v_tile[d][0..31]. Banks = 0,0,1,1,2,2,...,15,15. Same 2-way pattern, no real conflict. At n=32: threads 0..31 read v_tile[d][32..63]. Banks = 16,16,17,17,...,31,31. So the access pattern for V during PV multiply also has no real conflicts. Great! So transposed layout is the way to go. Wait, but for the p_smem reads during PV multiply: Thread tid reads p_smem[n] where n varies in the loop. p_smem[n] is at bank n/2 % 32 (assuming p_smem is in 2-byte elements? No, p_smem stores float, so 4 bytes each). If p_smem is float[BLOCK_N], address = n * 4. Bank = (n * 4) / 4 % 32 = n % 32. At n=0: thread 0 reads bank 0, thread 1 reads bank 1, ..., thread 31 reads bank 31. No conflicts! At n=1: thread 0 reads bank 1, thread 1 reads bank 2, ..., thread 31 reads bank 0. No conflicts! etc. This is perfect! No conflicts for p_smem either. OK, I'm now fairly convinced that using a transposed layout for K and V will give conflict-free shared memory access. Now, for the q_vec access during dot product: all threads in a warp read q_vec[d] for the SAME d at the same time. q_vec is in shared memory as float[128]. Address = d * 4. Bank = (d * 4 / 4) % 32 = d % 32. For d fixed, all threads in a warp read the SAME address q_vec[d]. This is a broadcast! No conflicts. Excellent. The memory access patterns are now clean. Actually, I realize my bandwidth calculation is somewhat wrong because K and V are read from shared memory after being loaded once. The global memory bandwidth is what matters for loading K and V tiles. Each block loads K and V once each per tile. Per block, per tile: K = 128*128*2 = 32KB, V = 32KB.
